   * Subscriber API for the Fides CMP-applicability resolution. Invokes
   * `callback` exactly once with `{ gppApplies, tcfApplies }` describing which CMPs
   * (TCF, GPP) apply to the current user.
   *
   * Exposed publicly on `window.fidesUtils.onCmpResolved` so any code on the page
   * can subscribe regardless of when it loads relative to `fidesScript`.
   *
   * The two branches handle a timing problem inherent to fire-and-forget
   * browser events: `window.dispatchEvent` only delivers to listeners
   * registered *before* the dispatch — there is no buffering or replay for
   * late subscribers. Without explicit handling, any consumer that called
   * `onCmpResolved` after the publisher (`conveyFidesCmpResolution`) had already dispatched the
   * `FIDES_CMP_RESOLVED` event would hang forever waiting for an event
   * that already happened.
   *
   *   - **Late subscriber** (`lastCmpResolution !== undefined`): the resolution
   *     has already been published. Invoke `callback` synchronously with
   *     the captured `lastCmpResolution` payload.
   *
   *   - **On-time subscriber** (`lastCmpResolution === undefined`): the
   *     resolution has not yet been published. Register a one-time
   *     (`{ once: true }`) listener for `FIDES_CMP_RESOLVED`; when the
   *     event fires, forward `event.detail` to `callback`.
   *
   * `{ once: true }` matters because the publisher can dispatch
   * `FIDES_CMP_RESOLVED` more than once per page load — once per loaded
   * `<script>` tag's `onload` (TCF, GPP stub, GPP), and again on each
   * `loadFides` re-run (`initWebview:ios`, `purrCacheRefreshed`). Without
   * `{ once: true }` an on-time subscriber would have its callback invoked
   * multiple times for what is conceptually a single resolution event.
   *
   * Note: the two branches return data from different sources. The on-time
   * path replays `event.detail` (the original dispatched payload). The
   * late path *recomputes* the payload from `pageCookies`. These produce
   * the same result today only because `pageCookies` happens not to change
   * between dispatch and replay — a future change that updates
   * `pageCookies` outside `loadFides` would create a real divergence.
   *
   * @param callback - Invoked once with `{ gppApplies, tcfApplies }` indicating
   *   which CMPs apply to this user, or `undefined` if a custom
   *   `FIDES_CMP_RESOLVED` event was dispatched without `detail`.
   *//**
   * Reads the GPP (Global Privacy Platform) string from various sources and writes it to the fides_overrides object.
   *
   * The function attempts to retrieve the GPP string in the following priority order:
   * 1. Query parameter (gpp-string) when load-gpp=true is also present
   * 2. Window config (win.config.gppData.encodedString)
   * 3. Override cookie (gpp-string-override)
   * 4. Standard GPP cookie (gpp-string)
   *
   * @param cookieStr - The document cookie string to parse
   * @param shouldAlertOnMissingGppString - Whether to send a Sentry alert if no GPP string is found.
   *   Set to `true` only when this is expected to be the final attempt to retrieve the GPP string
   *   (e.g., during initWebview:ios or purrCacheRefreshed events). Set to `false` during initial
   *   page load or other scenarios where the GPP string might legitimately not be available yet
   *   and additional calls are expected.
   *
   * @throws {Error} When no valid GPP string is found from any source
   */e(),d.addEventListener("initWebview:ios",function(){return e(!1,!0)},{once:!0}),document.addEventListener("purrCacheRefreshed",function(){return e(!0,!1)},{once:!0}),d.fidesUtils={shouldLoadTCF:p,shouldLoadGPP:o,onCmpResolved:function b(a){f?a(f):d.addEventListener("FIDES_CMP_RESOLVED",function(b){a(b.detail)},{once:!0})}}})('https://static01.nyt.com/vi-assets/static-assets/fides-1182b3743d46b6ae880fe217339d4333.js', 'https://static01.nyt.com/vi-assets/static-assets/fides-gpp-5a027aa4b3607787ee6c96c4967fc45a.js', 'https://static01.nyt.com/vi-assets/static-assets/iab-gpp-stub-02a14dbfddf7bec445adfaeef0a17830.js', window);

    const MKTG_KEY = 'mktg';
    const SUB_KEY = 'sub';
    const TEST_CLIENTSIDE_KEY = 'als_test_clientside';
    const ALS_DATA_KEY = 'ALS_DATA';
    const { origin, pathname, host, search } = window?.location || {};
    const alice_rules_test = new URLSearchParams(search).get('alice_rules_test');
    const isMobile = window.matchMedia('(width < 740px)').matches;
    const params = {
      alice_rules_test,

      prop: 'wc',
      plat: isMobile ? 'mweb' : 'web',
      typ:'revre'
    }
    function setKVPValues (data) {
      return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
        window.googletag = window.googletag || {};
        // Flatten the nested data into a single targeting map. User is expanded
        // after Asset so user-based KVPs take priority if keys ever collide.
        const { Asset, User, als_test_clientside } = data || {};
        const adTargetValues = {
          ...Asset,
          ...User,
          ...(als_test_clientside ? { als_test_clientside } : {})
        };
        function setTargeting (targets) {
          if (!targets) return;
          Object.keys(targets).forEach(function(key) {
            if (targets[key] != null) {
              window.googletag.pubads().setTargeting(key, targets[key]);
            }
          });
        }
        if (window?.googletag?.apiReady) {
          setTargeting(adTargetValues);
        } else {
          window.googletag.cmd = window.googletag.cmd || [];
          function onGPT() {
            setTargeting(adTargetValues);
          };
          window.googletag.cmd.push(onGPT);
        }
        resolve(data);
      });
    }
    function getLocalStorageValues() {
      const alsData = JSON.parse(window.localStorage.getItem(ALS_DATA_KEY) || '{}');
      const { User = {}, als_test_clientside } = alsData;
      return {
        [TEST_CLIENTSIDE_KEY]: als_test_clientside || null,
        User: {
          [MKTG_KEY]: null,
          [BT_KEY]: null,
          [SUB_KEY]: null,
          ...User,
        },
      }
    }
    // set starting values from previous LS values
    setKVPValues(getLocalStorageValues()).catch(function(e) {
      console.warn('SOMETHING TRAGIC HAPPENED', e);
    });
    function mapServiceDataToKVPs(data = { User: {}, Asset: {} }) {
      const { User = {}, Asset = {}, als_test_clientside } = data;

      // Pass the whole User object through (ALS now strips sensitive values).
      // Keep the data nested so we can store User separately from per-page Asset data.
      return {
        Asset,
        User,
        ...(als_test_clientside ? { als_test_clientside } : {})
      };
    }
    function getService (url) {
      return window.fetch(url, { credentials: 'include' })
      .then(function (response) {
        return response.json()
      })
      .then(mapServiceDataToKVPs)
      .catch(function(e) {
        console.warn('FETCH ISSUES!',e);
      });
    }
    function setLocalStorageValues (data) {
      // Ignore Asset-based data for local storage since user data should be
      // the same across different assets.
      const { User, als_test_clientside } = data || {};
      if (User || als_test_clientside) {
        try {
          localStorage.setItem(
            ALS_DATA_KEY,
            JSON.stringify({
              User,
              ...(als_test_clientside ? { als_test_clientside: 'ls-'+als_test_clientside } : {})
            })
          );
        } catch (e) {
          return console.warn(e);
        }
      }
    }
    function getServiceUrl (host, params) {
      function filterParams ([,value]) {
        return value !== null && value !== undefined;
      }
      const baseUrl = host === 'www.nytimes.com' ? PRD_HOST : DEV_HOST;
      const url = new URL(baseUrl);
      const filteredParams = Object.entries(params || {}).filter(filterParams);
      url.search = new URLSearchParams(filteredParams).toString();
      return url.href;
    }
    getService(getServiceUrl(host, params)).then(setKVPValues).then(setLocalStorageValues).catch(console.warn);

c882c9ce"/></div></a></div><div class="cdb485da"><p class="a90f7598">By<!-- --> <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/authors/rachel-cericola/" class="e31bedc8" data-gtm-trigger="author_name_clicks" data-author-name="Rachel Cericola" data-scp="author_name">Rachel Cericola</a></p><p class="c8779cf9" data-scp="author_byline">Rachel Cericola is a writer covering smart home. In addition to testing hundreds of smart devices, she has also tested dozens of ice cream sandwiches.</p></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></header></div><div class="f21e64f1 b5823c44 a3bb01fc dfed01f2 f8687b84"><aside class="d4496375 c893684a" data-scp="hidden"></aside></div><div id="intro" class="b9c067b5 eb5163eb f21e64f1 b5823c44 cdde21c0 ba640706 a7fb3038"><section class="e60ecf56" data-gtm-element="lede"><p>Change is hard, and many people resent it, but the notion of moving away from home is especially fraught, emotionally and financially. That may help explain the trend of older adults’ choosing to age in place at home, rather than in a retirement community or assisted-living facility. In a 2024 AARP report, <a href="https://press.aarp.org/2024-12-10-New-AARP-Report-Majority-Adults-50-plus-Age-Place-Policies-Communities-Catch-Up" rel="noopener" target="_blank">75% of surveyed Americans ages 50 and older</a> said they planned to ride out the golden years at home.</p><p>Aging in place has obvious appeal, but it also may pose substantial challenges. Fortunately, staying put through old age can be made easier thanks in part to a wide range of technological advances. Internet-connected DIY devices, such as smart window shades, light bulbs, security cameras, pill dispensers, and smoke alarms, provide a reliable buffer against unknown complications. And some tech devices are powerful problem-solvers that can counterbalance the inevitable reduction in capability as a person ages. They can also enhance convenience and comfort while boosting safety and peace of mind.</p><p>Anne Madison, a 75-year-old Baltimore resident, has <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/smart-home-devices-independent-living/">a growing collection of smart-home devices</a>. Smart lights illuminate dark areas to prevent falls, while other devices remind her about important medications and appointments. Some even allow her to get in touch with emergency services if needed. “I think these little devices are helping to keep me independent and out of an assisted-living or nursing-home facility,” she said.</p><p>With the help of experts and smart-device owners, along with our years of testing and reviewing, we’ve compiled this collection of devices and services that are especially well suited to helping aging adults live more independently, with increased safety and comfort. And for people who rely on a caregiver, these tools can provide feedback on daily activities, instant communication, and a better sense of security.</p></section><div class="ece5cf85"><button fill="black"></path></svg><div class="b8b03d91"><div class="d7834fa9" data-scp="highlights_title" data-testid="highlights-title">Arrange tech support</div><div class="c4edb388" data-scp="highlights_summary" data-testid="highlights-summary"><p>Most of these devices require using a smartphone. If you aren’t tech-savvy, enlist a caregiver or a pro service to help with installation and device management.</p> </div></div></li><li class="e6b94699" data-scp="highlights_list_item" data-testid="highlights-list-item"><svg width="22" height="20" viewBox="0 0 20 20" fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" data-icon="circle-checkmark"><path fill-rule="evenodd" data-scp="highlights_title" data-testid="highlights-title">Organize controls</div><div class="c4edb388" data-scp="highlights_summary" data-testid="highlights-summary"><p>If you have several devices that require an app, you can usually group them using a single compatible app, such as Alexa, Google Home, or Apple Home.</p> </div></div></li><li class="e6b94699" data-scp="highlights_list_item" data-testid="highlights-list-item"><svg width="22" height="20" viewBox="0 0 20 20" fill="none" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" data-icon="circle-checkmark"><path fill-rule="evenodd" data-scp="highlights_title" data-testid="highlights-title">Emergency contacts</div><div class="c4edb388" data-scp="highlights_summary" data-testid="highlights-summary"><p>Some devices, such as alarms, can be configured to automatically text or call a relative or other third party, as well as 911.</p> </div></div></li></ul></div></div></div><div class="a1497183"><div class="f21e64f1 b5823c44 cdde21c0"></div></div><div class="a1497183"><div class="ab72109b f21e64f1 b5823c44 bdfcf243"><div class="c3dc8b66"></div><div><div class="cdb42bcc"><div class="e1bc3c12"><section id="table-of-contents" class="ffed1540" data-scp="removed" data-testid="toc" data-gtm-element="toc"><header class="d4645bc9"><h2 class="b6525d53">The research</h2></header></section></div></div><div class="cdb42bcc"><div class="e1bc3c12"><section class="b6d81848 d47f7b03" id="why-you-should-trust-us"><h2 class="e823fba7" aria-controls="why-you-should-trust-us-panel" aria-selected="true" tabindex="-1" data-gtm-element="chapter_heading" data-gtm-trigger="single_chapter" data-chapter-name="Why you should trust us" data-expand-close="close" data-chapter-index="1">Why you should trust us</h2><div id="why-you-should-trust-us-panel" class="fa265be5" aria-labelledby="why-you-should-trust-us" aria-hidden="false"><p>I’m a writer covering the smart home for Wirecutter. I have been writing about technology for more than 25 years, and I have written guides to <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-wireless-outdoor-home-security-camera/">outdoor security cameras</a>, <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-smart-speakers/">smart speakers</a>, and <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-smart-bird-feeder/">smart bird feeders</a>.</p><p>For this guide:</p><ul><li>I’ve tested more than 650 different smart-home products.</li><li>I’ve interviewed more than 15 device users, caregivers, doctors, and other experts.</li><li>In accordance with <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/editorial-standards/">Wirecutter standards</a>, our staffers return or donate all products we’ve tested once our assessment of them is complete (this may involve <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/why-wirecutter-tests-products-for-years/">longer-term testing</a> conducted by my colleagues and me). We <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/blog/yes-i-work-at-wirecutter-no-we-dont-get-a-bunch-of-free-stuff/">never hang on to “freebies”</a> once testing is done.</li></ul></div></section></div></div><div class="cdb42bcc"><div class="e1bc3c12"><section class="b6d81848 d47f7b03" id="who-this-is-for"><h2 class="e823fba7" aria-controls="who-this-is-for-panel" aria-selected="true" tabindex="-1" data-gtm-element="chapter_heading" data-gtm-trigger="single_chapter" data-chapter-name="Who this is for" data-expand-close="close" data-chapter-index="2">Who this is for</h2><div id="who-this-is-for-panel" class="fa265be5" aria-labelledby="who-this-is-for" aria-hidden="false"><p>This guide is for the person wanting to age in place, as well as for family members, friends, or caregivers who support an older adult’s decision to live independently. Tech devices can’t be a complete replacement for human care, but many can reliably assist when caregivers aren’t able to be around in person. And unlike people, technologies provide round-the-clock, 24/7 monitoring and a connection to ensure the safety of a loved one. They can also assist with daily tasks and scheduling, and they make it easier for others to check in for peace of mind. Some of these devices include:</p><ul><li>home security cameras for communicating or for monitoring who is coming and going</li><li>smart lighting to illuminate dark hallways and entryways</li><li>medication dispensers to guarantee that the correct pills are being taken at the right time of day</li></ul><p>“You’re really empowering someone to live ways that they’ve never been able to live before based on their chronic conditions,” said Andy Miller, senior vice president of innovation and product development at <a href="https://www.aarp.org/" rel="noopener" target="_blank">AARP</a>. (Note: AARP has partnered with some tech manufacturers to offer discounts on select devices and services.)</p><p>If you’re buying and setting up devices for yourself, you will need to be comfortable using smartphone apps and doing some lightweight installation, and you may need to do a bit of low-level technical troubleshooting, such as adjusting Wi-Fi settings. If you’re not up for that sort of thing, you will need to depend on a family member, a friend, a caregiver, or potentially a professional service to get things up and running (and, occasionally, back up and running).</p><p>Steve Mullen, who co-hosts the <a href="https://caregivinggenxstyle.podbean.com/" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><em>Caregiving Gen X Style</em></a> podcast near Richmond, Virginia, with his wife Jennifer, said he purchased an <a href="#smart-speakers-and-screens">Amazon Echo</a> device and <a href="#smart-plugs">smart plugs</a> to help his 85-year-old mother, who is in a wheelchair. “There were a lot of growing pains along the way,” he said. “It works fine now, because she’s got it down. She’s been doing this for almost four years now. But for the first year or so, there were some hiccups.”</p><p>If a caregiver intends to use tech devices to help in the care of someone else, the caregiver and the older person should work in collaboration. The experts we spoke with were emphatic about this. The person being cared for should be fully informed as to what devices are being used and where, what they do, and how they work. And the caregiver should explain how these devices can help them feel safer and more comfortable, and even live a more independent life.</p><p>Also, the decision to use technology necessarily raises security and privacy concerns. At Wirecutter, <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/blog/smart-home-security-privacy/">we vet all of our device and service picks</a> to confirm that they abide by standard safety practices. Whatever <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/blog/how-to-protect-your-smart-home-from-hackers/">privacy sacrifices</a> you may incur by using the devices and services we recommend, we believe that, based on our experience, the real-world benefits outweigh any likely risks.</p><p>“I don’t worry about privacy, because it’s already gone straight to hell,” Anne Madison told us. “These people are smarter than I am. I just don’t think about it much. I’m more worried about turning on my lights. That’s where I assign more worries.”</p></div></section></div></div><div class="cdb42bcc"><div class="e1bc3c12"><section class="b6d81848 d47f7b03" id="how-we-picked-and-tested"><h2 class="e823fba7" aria-controls="how-we-picked-and-tested-panel" aria-selected="true" tabindex="-1" data-gtm-element="chapter_heading" data-gtm-trigger="single_chapter" data-chapter-name="How we picked and tested" data-expand-close="close" data-chapter-index="3">How we picked and tested</h2><div id="how-we-picked-and-tested-panel" class="fa265be5" aria-labelledby="how-we-picked-and-tested" aria-hidden="false"><p>This guide is the product of several years of testing and reporting, as well as conversations and interviews with a range of manufacturers, doctors, caregivers, experts, and device owners.</p><p>Some of these selections may not be our overall top picks in their respective guides, since older people and their caregivers may be better off with a different option that meets their specific needs.</p><p>Claire Perlman, Wirecutter’s editor of accessibility and aging in place, noted that it’s important to recognize that most devices are not one-size-fits-all. “The spectrum of ages and abilities of those aging in place varies so widely that it’s difficult to choose just one device for such a broad community,” she said. “Is the goal to help the older person control their immediate environment or the ambience of their home? To help a long-distance caretaker know that their loved one is okay? I think it depends on the individual’s goals more than anything else.”</p><p>For each section of this guide, we looked for devices that offered the following features and attributes:</p><ul><li><strong>Simple operation:</strong> Ideally, tech devices are intuitive to use and allow operation via some combination of manual controls, apps, or voice commands.</li><li><strong>Wireless connectivity:</strong> Most of these devices require a Wi-Fi connection to function. If you’re planning to use multiple devices or any that stream video, first confirm that the person’s home has sufficiently speedy Wi-Fi. If the home has reception issues, consider investing in a <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-wi-fi-extender/">wireless extender</a> or a <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-wi-fi-mesh-networking-kits/">Wi-Fi mesh-networking kit</a>.</li><li><strong>Safety:</strong> We considered a lot of different types of devices for this guide but focused primarily on those that enhance safety and security, factors that are of particular concern for older people living at home and especially those living alone.</li><li><strong>Unobtrusive design:</strong> We looked for devices that wouldn’t clutter a home and could function without interrupting everyday life. That included devices that could work in a traditional manner, such as a light switch or doorbell, while also supporting smart features.</li><li><strong>Smart-home functions:</strong> Many of the devices we selected work with iOS or Android devices, but if you intend to have several devices work together, first confirm that they support the same smart-home software platforms. The most popular platforms are Amazon Alexa, Apple Home, Google Home, <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/what-you-need-to-know-about-matter/">Matter</a>, and Samsung SmartThings. Many devices allow you to control them using at least one and often several of those platforms simultaneously.</li></ul></div></section></div></div><div class="cdb42bcc"><div class="e1bc3c12"><section class="b6d81848 d47f7b03" id="health-and-safety"><h2 class="e823fba7" aria-controls="health-and-safety-panel" aria-selected="true" tabindex="-1" data-gtm-element="chapter_heading" data-gtm-trigger="single_chapter" data-chapter-name="Health and safety" data-expand-close="close" data-chapter-index="4">Health and safety</h2><div id="health-and-safety-panel" class="fa265be5" aria-labelledby="health-and-safety" aria-hidden="false"><p>When you’re making an aging-in-place plan, health and personal safety should be a top priority. You can find tech devices to help with everything from hearing sounds to taking daily medications to ensuring that someone is staying active and out of danger. And if you’re a caregiver, these devices make it easier to monitor loved ones, whether they’re on the other side of town or the other side of the country.</p><h3>Emergency-contact systems</h3><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" data-scp="callout"><div id="callout-chapter-7-1" class="fd1ac384" data-scp="callout_item" data-testid="callout" data-pid="57280"><div class="f1cabe17" data-gtm-context="inline" tabindex="-1">UnaliWear Kanega Medical Alert Watch</a></h3></div><p class="ec57f3ca" data-scp="callout_description">This smartwatch-style system is stress-free to set up and use. But it’s expensive, and during testing its GPS wasn’t always accurate.</p><div class="dded66b7" data-testid="buy-product-box"><div data-scp="removed"><div class="b279b202 product-pricebox-0" data-source-id-order="0" data-country-code="US"><a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/57280/193152/4/245979?merchant=UnaliWear" class="b50e01ee" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-element="callout_buy_button_impression" data-index="1" data-link-type="button" data-merchant="UnaliWear" data-pick-type-id="7" data-pid="57280" data-store="UnaliWear" data-vars-store="UnaliWear" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/57280/193152/4/245979?merchant=UnaliWear" data-gtm-context="inline" data-gtm-trigger="callout_product_link_button"><span class="ce5e4615">Buying options: </span><span>$299</span> from <span>UnaliWear</span><span class="ce5e4615">, opens in a new tab</span></a></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><p><strong>How it helps:</strong> According to the <a href="https://www.cdcfoundation.org/programs/falls" rel="noopener" target="_blank">CDC Foundation</a>, more than one in four people 65 and older fall each year. <a href="https://www.cdc.gov/falls/about/index.html" rel="noopener" target="_blank">It’s the leading cause of injury</a> for that age group, with over 38,000 deaths recorded from falls in 2021 alone. Having a way to contact emergency services is an absolute must. “A device that can be worn on the wrist allows for 24/7 monitoring and can help identify against the biggest and most common concern, which is a fall,” said David Siegel, CEO of <a href="https://www.nationwidemedical.com/" rel="noopener" target="_blank">Nationwide Medical</a>. “A wrist-worn device can also provide important data, detect falls, determine location, and even serve as a telephonic or text communication device.”</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick: </strong>The <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/57280/193152/4/245997/?merchant=UnaliWear" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="UnaliWear" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/57280/193152/4/245997/?merchant=UnaliWear" data-vars-store="UnaliWear" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="UnaliWear" data-pid="57280" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/57280/193152/4/245997/?merchant=UnaliWear" data-gtm-context="chapter_health-and-safety">UnaliWear Kanega Medical Alert Watch</a> doesn’t look like an emergency device, but it acts like one, providing on-the-go care both inside and outside the home. It relies on the Verizon network but also connects to Wi-Fi (and we found that the GPS worked best in that mode). When an emergency arises, just push a button or verbally tell the watch using set wake words; from there, you’re connected to a live operator. For times when you can’t do that, the Kanega watch has fall detection. <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/the-best-medical-alert-systems/">In our testing</a>, we found that agents would call a cell phone if the user couldn’t hear them clearly through the watch. The Kanega watch also includes medication reminders, and because it comes with a pair of swappable rechargeable batteries, you never have to worry about being without it for charging.</p><figure However, it can be used anywhere within range of a cellular signal, not just in your home like the less expensive LifeFone Safe Watch Active (more information on that and other options below).</p><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" data-scp="callout"><div id="callout-chapter-7-2" class="fd1ac384" data-scp="callout_item" data-testid="callout" data-pid="57807"><div class="f1cabe17" This paid service ($6 per month or $59 per year) relies on your having an Amazon Echo voice-controlled smart speaker, through which you can connect to live responders who assist in calling emergency responders and relaying your medical information.</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> Of the medical alert systems we tested, Alexa Emergency Assist had the fastest response time. In addition to connecting with live emergency services, the service can notify up to 25 of your emergency contacts. Alexa Emergency Assist works with all <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-alexa-speakers/">Amazon Echo</a> smart speakers and screens, but we like the <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52377/184062/4/184694/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52377/184062/4/184694/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="52377" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52377/184062/4/184694/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_health-and-safety">Amazon Echo Dot</a> for this purpose because it’s small and inexpensive. If you aren’t familiar with smart speakers, it may involve a bit of a learning curve, but once you get used to it, Alexa has other useful features, including the ability to act as an intercom, allowing family, friends, and caregivers with Echo devices or the Alexa app to “<a href="https://amazon.com/gp/help/customer/display.html?nodeId=GS3WRTSRKD2U6MCK" rel="noopener" target="_blank">drop in</a>” for an instant two-way conversation.</p><p>Anne Madison has been using it since 2023. She told us that the service works well, and she’s “now a firm believer in it” after a fall in the bedroom left her unable to get up. “I said something along the lines of ’Alexa, call for help,’ and an actual human operator answered, listened to my problem, and indicated that they would call 911,” she said. Paramedics arrived within a few minutes and suggested that she call a relative. In the chaos, Anne misplaced her phone, but a quick “Alexa, call my phone” helped her locate it just as swiftly.</p><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" data-scp="callout"><div While the cellular model works independently like a phone (and has its own number and cell plan), the less expensive base-model Apple Watch needs to be paired with an iPhone that’s always within Association</a>, 59% of home fire deaths result from the absence of a smoke alarm or the presence of a faulty one. Everyone should have working smoke detectors throughout the house, but there’s a particular concern as to whether someone with reduced hearing will notice an alarm — and how quickly they might react to it. <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-smart-smoke-alarm/">Smart smoke detectors</a> not only deliver an audible siren inside the house but can also send alerts to friends, fetchPriority="auto"/></picture></span></span><figcaption class="caption-text"> <!-- --> <span class="caption-text caption-credit">Marki Williams/NYT Wirecutter</span></figcaption></figure><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> The <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/75373/226940/4/246000/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/75373/226940/4/246000/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="75373" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/75373/226940/4/246000/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_health-and-safety">Kidde Smart Smoke + Carbon Monoxide Alarm</a> is a hardwired model that can detect both smoke and carbon monoxide. When the device detects danger, it sends a smartphone alert to the owners so that they are alerted while home or away, as well as to any other designated contacts. If it turns out that someone is just cooking up bacon, you can easily silence the alarm through the Kidde app. And because it interconnects with hardwired non-smart Kidde detectors, every detector in the house can have the same smart features. Note that this hardwired model still requires backup batteries (that’s a good thing) and issues an alert when those are getting low.</p><p><strong>Another option to consider:</strong> If you want emergency services to be available, consider the <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/70601/230603/4/246001/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/70601/230603/4/246001/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="70601" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/70601/230603/4/246001/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_health-and-safety">Kidde Ring Smart Smoke and CO Alarm</a>. This model is almost identical to the above Kidde detector, except it works with the standalone $5-per-month <a href="https://ring.com/carbon-monoxide-smoke-monitoring" rel="noopener" target="_blank">24/7 Smoke and CO Professional Monitoring plan</a> or a <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/33728/155317/4/246002/?merchant=Ring" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Ring" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/33728/155317/4/246002/?merchant=Ring" data-vars-store="Ring" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Ring" data-pid="33728" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/33728/155317/4/246002/?merchant=Ring" data-gtm-context="chapter_health-and-safety">Ring Protect plan</a> (if you have the <a href="#home-security-system">Ring Alarm</a>). Unlike the other version, this model uses the Ring app. Also, you can’t silence it in the app: You need to push a physical button on the device (unless the smoke subsides first).</p><h3>Hearing href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-over-the-counter-hearing-aids/">Over-the-counter hearing aids</a> are a slightly more affordable option specifically designed for people who have mild to moderate hearing loss and are able to clearly communicate feedback. These devices don’t require a prescription and can be set up by the wearer, a family member, or a caregiver. Our headphones expert, writer Lauren Dragan, advises that anyone with more severe hearing loss or who needs sound processing that bypasses the inner ear should consult a medical professional instead of trying to use an OTC hearing aid.</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> The <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/59223/211079/4/246003/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/59223/211079/4/246003/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="59223" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/59223/211079/4/246003/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_health-and-safety">Jabra Enhance Select 300</a> is easy to set up, customize, and use, thanks to the Jabra app, which includes a video orientation section with an audio professional. We found this set to be more adjustable than most, with options to fine-tune the volume, as well as to balance treble, midrange, and bass. Instead of using replaceable button batteries, this set runs on rechargeable batteries, which provide about 15 hours of use. <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-over-the-counter-hearing-aids/">We found</a> that the earpieces were good at staying in place and also provided a pretty comfortable expensive options.</p><h3>Smart pill dispensers</h3><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" data-scp="callout"><div id="callout-chapter-7-6" class="fd1ac384" data-scp="callout_item" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/72081/234713/4/245984?merchant=Hero%20Health" class="fa8c1483 product-merchant-link-button" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" data-pid="72081" data-store="Hero Health" data-vars-store="Hero Health" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/72081/234713/4/245984?merchant=Hero%20Health" data-gtm-trigger="callout_product_link_name" data-scp="callout_product" data-gtm-context="inline" tabindex="-1">Hero Smart Pill Dispenser</a></h3></div><p class="ec57f3ca" data-scp="callout_description">This app-dependent dispenser reminds you when it’s time to take your meds, dispenses them on a schedule, and keeps caregivers in the loop.</p><div class="dded66b7" data-testid="buy-product-box"><div data-scp="removed"><div class="b279b202 product-pricebox-0" data-source-id-order="0" data-country-code="US"><a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/72081/234713/4/245984?merchant=Hero%20Health" class="b50e01ee" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-element="callout_buy_button_impression" data-index="6" data-link-type="button" data-merchant="Hero Health" data-pick-type-id="7" data-pid="72081" data-store="Hero Health" data-gtm-trigger="callout_product_link_button"><span class="ce5e4615">Buying options: </span>Buy<!-- --> from <span>Hero Health</span><span class="ce5e4615">, opens in a new tab</span></a></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><p><strong>How it helps:</strong> If you take more than one pill a day, a <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-pill-boxes/">pill box</a> is a must. However, you have to fill the box weekly (or sometimes more often), and some people need an extra nudge to take their medications. A smart pill dispenser can organize several weeks’ worth of meds and can ensure that the right dose is being dispensed at precisely the right times.</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> The <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/72081/234713/4/246005/?merchant=Hero%20Health" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Hero Health" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/72081/234713/4/246005/?merchant=Hero%20Health" data-vars-store="Hero Health" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Hero Health" data-pid="72081" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/72081/234713/4/246005/?merchant=Hero%20Health" data-gtm-context="chapter_health-and-safety">Hero Smart Pill Dispenser</a> can hold up to 90 days’ worth of 10 different medications. When it’s pill-taking time, the Hero plays both audible and visual alerts. Then you simply press a button on the device itself to have your meds dispensed. If someone skips a day or a dose, the Hero sends a smartphone alert to the user but also to anyone else you designate, through the companion app. It can also remind users to take up to 10 additional meds that aren’t stored in the device. One downside is that the Hero requires a one-year commitment to a monthly $45 subscription (or $30 per month if you pay annually).</p><p><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/hero-smart-pill-dispenser-review/">Our tester</a> found the Hero to be reliable and said that while the cost was covered by insurance, the price out of pocket would still be a lot less than paying someone to manage and monitor medications.</p></div></section></div></div><div class="cdb42bcc"><div class="e1bc3c12"><section class="b6d81848 d47f7b03" id="security"><h2 class="e823fba7" aria-controls="security-panel" aria-selected="true" tabindex="-1" data-gtm-element="chapter_heading" data-gtm-trigger="single_chapter" data-chapter-name="Security" data-expand-close="close" data-chapter-index="5">Security</h2><div id="security-panel" class="fa265be5" aria-labelledby="security" aria-hidden="false"><p>The following devices are helpful for people who want to continue to live independently but are concerned about security or may need to be monitored a bit more closely. Whatever devices you decide on, older adults and caregivers should have a discussion in advance about what devices will be installed, what information they can collect, and who will have access to them and control of them.</p><h3 id="home-security-system">Home security system</h3><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" data-scp="callout"><div id="callout-chapter-7-7" class="fd1ac384" data-scp="callout_item" data-testid="callout" data-pid="38230"><div class="f1cabe17" id="product-38230"><div class="f31f6df1"><div class="efc734d0"><a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/38230/161502/4/245985?merchant=Amazon" class="fa8c1483 product-merchant-link-button" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" Such systems include relatively inconspicuous sensors that let you know when a door or window has been left open. Security cameras notify you when visitors of any sort come, and they also record when deliveries arrive. Some systems offer other helpful add-on devices, such as water-leak sensors, glass-break sensors, and panic buttons. Conveniently, the systems also allow you to manage all of these devices using a single smartphone app (instead of juggling several).</p><p><a href="http://nytimes.com/wirecutter/blog/professionally-monitored-security-system/" rel="noopener" target="_blank">We strongly recommend</a> adding a professional monitoring plan to a security system so that in the event of an alarm, a live person from a call center will alert you directly — and if you’re unreachable, they’ll notify anyone else you’ve deemed an emergency contact. tab</span></a></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><p><strong>How it helps:</strong> A <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-smart-doorbell-camera/">smart doorbell camera</a> allows you to screen whoever comes to the door, from anywhere in the world, regardless of whether the visitor even rings the bell. When someone rings the bell, you hear it at home but also receive a smartphone notification, which allows you to view in real time who is at your door. But even if someone drops by just to deliver a package, motion sensors will still detect them and alert you. From there, you can opt to answer the door remotely or just keep a record of who is coming and going — and how long they hang around.</p><p>“Video doorbells are great, especially if [the person living with them is] having mobility challenges,” said William Dale, MD, PhD, professor and chair of supportive care medicine at the Center for Cancer and Aging at <a href="https://www.cityofhope.org/" rel="noopener" target="_blank">City of Hope</a>. “Maybe the house is a little big for them, or they have to be in a far part of the house, and getting all the way to the doors becomes a challenge.”</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> The <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/76953/230440/4/246009/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/76953/230440/4/246009/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="76953" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/76953/230440/4/246009/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_security">TP-Link Tapo D130 Wired Video Doorbell</a> delivers a clear 2K image and can distinguish whether the action happening in front of it is due to a person, animal, vehicle, package, or general motion. It even comes with a plug-in chime that you can place in any outlet anywhere in the house. It’s easy to use, and it’s also great for someone on a budget, because the hardware costs less than $50 and recordings can be stored to a microSD card for free. Or you can pay for <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/53351/185918/4/246855/?merchant=Tapo" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Tapo" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/53351/185918/4/246855/?merchant=Tapo" data-vars-store="Tapo" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Tapo" data-pid="53351" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/53351/185918/4/246855/?merchant=Tapo" data-gtm-context="chapter_security">cloud-based storage</a>, starting at $3.50 per month for one camera, so you never have to worry about someone stealing the card or a power outage affecting your storage.</p><p><strong>Another option to consider:</strong> If you don’t have existing doorbell wiring, the <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/63955/204947/4/246857/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/63955/204947/4/246857/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="63955" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/63955/204947/4/246857/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_security">TP-Link Tapo D225</a> is an excellent battery-operated 2K doorbell camera with many of the same features as the hardwired D130. At 5.91 by 1.97 by 1.52 inches, it&#x27;s much more chunky than the D130. That&#x27;s due to the internal battery, which lasts about four months, depending on how much activity it captures.</p><h3>Smart indoor cameras</h3><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" tab</span></a></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><p><strong>How it helps:</strong> An indoor camera provides peace of mind when you’re not home, whether you want to monitor your property or your pet. For caregivers, it allows you to remotely monitor home visits and movement, as well as whether a person is taking their medicine. And for families, it also allows you to monitor the level of care your loved one is receiving.</p><p>“I have a lot of families with cameras,” said Vikki Levine, director of client services at <a href="https://www.ezrahomecare.com/" rel="noopener" target="_blank">Ezra Home Care</a>. “In a positive way, it’s affecting the caliber of work that our caregivers are doing. They’re not getting away with sitting on their phone, because I’ll know about it faster than fast.”</p><p>All the experts we spoke with were adamant that installing <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-wi-fi-home-security-camera/">indoor security cameras</a> needs to be a decision made between you, your loved one, and any outside help. “Transparency should be obvious. Nobody likes to be monitored without knowing,” said City of Hope’s Dale. “These kinds of things have to be really carefully thought about rather than just assumed that it’s okay.” Talk to the person living with the camera about why it’s important, how it works, and how you can use it to communicate, since most cameras come equipped with a speaker and microphone.</p><figure id="attachment_426527" incidents, according to the <a href="https://www.iii.org/fact-statistic/facts-statistics-homeowners-and-renters-insurance#:~:text=About%20one%20in%2040%20insured,due%20to%20theft%20each%20year." rel="noopener" target="_blank">Insurance Information Institute</a>. Leaks often happen in hard-to-notice and even harder-to-reach areas, such as under a sink, behind the washing machine, or from the water heater in the basement. <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/the-best-smart-leak-detector/">Smart water-leak sensors</a> can help by sending you a smartphone alert the instant they detect water. The best models have a loud alarm and the ability to integrate with smart-home platforms so that you can receive smart alerts. We also prefer when alerts can be silenced in the app, so you don’t need to physically access the detector (or wade through water) to stop an alarm.</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> The <a screens</a> make keeping in touch easier and more personal than a simple phone call. These devices connect to your home’s Wi-Fi network, allowing for video and voice calls to family, friends, telemedicine appointments, and emergency services; you can also set alarms, reminders, and more. These devices are easy to control with voice commands, so you can call up news, weather forecasts, recipes, sports scores, and trivia, too. For homes with smart-home devices such as light bulbs, security cameras, and smart plugs, you can use a smart speaker to control them using your voice.</p><p>Caregiver Kat Carney told us that she has always had problems waking up her 84-year-old mother. Then she purchased an Alexa device for their shared Marietta, Georgia, home. “She gets up when Alexa tells her to get up,” she said, noting that she also uses the devices to set <a href="https://amazon.com/gp/help/customer/display.html?nodeId=GDDXYWQUKGGWG79Y" rel="noopener" target="_blank">Reminders</a> about appointments and medications. “Alexa is my best friend.”</p><figure stream music, radio, and audiobooks. In addition, they’re an especially useful way to control smart devices that otherwise may be inconvenient or challenging to interact with. The <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/74837/226006/4/231375/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/74837/226006/4/231375/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="74837" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/74837/226006/4/231375/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_communications">Amazon Echo Show 11</a> also has a 11-inch HD touchscreen, so you can see and read information as a supplement to voice controls and audio. On top of that, its 13-megapixel camera turns its screen into a straightforward video-chat device, so you can stay in touch with family, friends, and caregivers via Alexa Calling as well as third-party options like Zoom. The Echo Show also has a “drop-in” two-way video- or audio-chat feature for instant conversation that works just like a two-way intercom. (If only one party has an Alexa device, that’s okay: Video chats also work with the Alexa app on any smartphone.)</p><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" data-scp="callout"><div highly recommended investing in a <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/the-ipad-is-the-best-tablet/">tablet</a> because they are generally portable and can provide easy access to news, entertainment, and even the outside world via text, email, and video chat. A tablet can also be more intuitive and comfortable to use than a laptop, since it functions like a smartphone but with a much larger screen, so icons are easier to read, and touch controls are more forgiving.</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> If you’re accustomed to using a smartphone, you can easily transition to using an <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/69346/232641/4/246045/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/69346/232641/4/246045/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="69346" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/69346/232641/4/246045/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_communications">Apple iPad</a> to access the apps for all of the devices we mention elsewhere in this guide. Using both Siri and an iPad, you can combine the control of multiple smart-home devices with one voice command: For instance, you can say “Siri, turn on the living room” to power several lights simultaneously. And you can combine those lights with smart plugs, cameras, and other smart devices, as long as they support the Apple Home platform. (Note, though, that Siri voice commands and the Home platform are not compatible with as many smart-home devices as Alexa and Google Home are.)</p><p><strong>Another option to consider:</strong> If you want all of the features of Alexa but don’t want a screen (or camera), opt for a smart speaker like the <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52377/184062/4/184694/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52377/184062/4/184694/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="52377" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52377/184062/4/184694/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_communications">Amazon Echo Dot</a>. Slim (EP25)</span></a></h2></div><h3 class="b550f131 a1a87f29" aria-hidden="true"><a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52097/183585/4/245995?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="inline" tabindex="-1">TP-Link Kasa Smart Wi-Fi Plug Slim (EP25)</a></h3></div><p class="ec57f3ca" data-scp="callout_description">This easy-to-use plug adds remote on/off control to a table lamp or fan.</p><div class="dded66b7" data-testid="buy-product-box"><div data-scp="removed"><div class="b279b202 product-pricebox-0" data-source-id-order="0" data-country-code="US"><a data-pid="52097" data-store="Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/52097/183585/4/245995?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="inline" data-gtm-trigger="callout_product_link_button"><span class="ce5e4615">Buying options: </span><span>$42</span> from <span>Amazon</span><span class="ce5e4615">, opens in a new tab</span></a><p id="buy-desc-52097-183585-details" class="aa702ae0">(pack of four)</p></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><p><strong>How it helps:</strong> <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/reviews/best-smart-switch/">Smart plugs</a> let you transform the way you control traditional electrical devices, yet they are the most straightforward assistive devices around — just plug one into an outlet, pair it with a smartphone, and then plug a table lamp, fan, or other on/off device into the smart plug. You can turn any of those devices on or off using a smartphone app or a voice command to a smart speaker or your phone. Or, if you prefer, you can put those devices on a repeating schedule, just as you would a traditional lighting timer.</p><p>Dan Kosmowski, who cares for his 79-year-old father in Birmingham, Michigan, said that his father often got distracted by the TV and forgot to use the bathroom. “Because of his type of dementia, he’s easily distracted,” he said. He uses a smart plug to make sure the TV stays off after-hours. “I tried using the built-in timer on the remote control, but I never remembered to do it consistently,” Kosmowski said. “So we have a recurring power-off cycle set for 10, and then it comes back on at 7 or 8 in the morning.”</p><figure id="attachment_470350" class="caption alignnone full-width"><span class="a33cf4b7"><span It’s also compatible with all of the major smart-home platforms for voice control. An extra feature for anyone who is conservation-minded is that it monitors the energy usage of whatever device you plug into it, displaying information in watts and kilowatt-hours (or hours and minutes) in the app.</p><h3>Smart window shades</h3><div class="ebb615ab global-cob e1e566ad" data-scp="callout"><div id="callout-chapter-7-18" class="fd1ac384" data-scp="callout_item" are a godsend for anyone who has mobility or physical limitations. These shades are especially helpful if a dwelling has multiple windows, multiple floors, or windows in hard-to-reach places. You can manually control most models through an app or voice control; you can also put them on a daily schedule so that they automatically open every morning and close at dusk (or any other times you prefer).</p><p><strong>Why it’s a pick:</strong> <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/62668/235030/4/246017/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" data-affiliate-link="true" data-payload-expected="false" rel="noreferrer sponsored" data-gtm-trigger="product_link" data-store="Amazon" data-pick-type-id="7" data-vars-outbound-link="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/62668/235030/4/246017/?merchant=Amazon" data-vars-store="Amazon" data-gtm-element="product_link_impression" data-link-type="link" data-merchant="Amazon" data-pid="62668" data-url="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/62668/235030/4/246017/?merchant=Amazon" data-gtm-context="chapter_accessibility-and-convenience">SmartWings Motorized Light Filtering Roller Shades</a> are easy to set up and use. They’re whisper-quiet, and they have an optional five-channel remote control for people who don’t have a phone or smart speaker handy. Unlike most models, SmartWings shades don’t have a dedicated control app. But because they’re Matter-enabled, you can control them using any of the major third-party platforms, such as Alexa, Apple Home, or Google Home (though that also means you’ll need a Matter hub, such as a smart speaker). The shades are available in a range of colors and styles, including blackout fabric that not only fully darkens a room but in our tests also helped regulate a room’s temperature. If you prefer the look and feel of fabric, the company also makes <a target="_blank" href="https://www.nytimes.com/wirecutter/out/link/68414/235031/4/246018/?merchant=Amazon" class="product-link" Tech devices can’t be a complete replacement for human care, but many can reliably assist when caregivers aren’t able to be around in person. And unlike people, technologies provide round-the-clock, 24/7 monitoring and a connection to ensure the safety of a loved one. They can also assist with daily tasks and scheduling, and they make it easier for others to check in for peace of mind. Some of these devices include:  \thome security cameras for communicating or for monitoring who is coming and going  \tsmart lighting to illuminate dark hallways and entryways  \tmedication dispensers to guarantee that the correct pills are being taken at the right time of day “You’re really empowering someone to live ways that they’ve never been able to live before based on their chronic conditions,” said Andy Miller, senior vice president of innovation and product development at AARP. (Note: AARP has partnered with some tech manufacturers to offer discounts on select devices and services.) If you’re buying and setting up devices for yourself, you will need to be comfortable using smartphone apps and doing some lightweight installation, and you may need to do a bit of low-level technical troubleshooting, such as adjusting Wi-Fi settings. If you’re not up for that sort of thing, you will need to depend on a family member, a friend, a caregiver, or potentially a professional service to get things up and running (and, occasionally, back up and running). Steve Mullen, who co-hosts the Caregiving Gen X Style podcast near Richmond, Virginia, with his wife Jennifer, said he purchased an Amazon Echo device and smart plugs to help his 85-year-old mother, who is in a wheelchair. “There were a lot of growing pains along the way,” he said. “It works fine now, because she’s got it down. She’s been doing this for almost four years now. But for the first year or so, there were some hiccups.” If a caregiver intends to use tech devices to help in the care of someone else, the caregiver and the older person should work in collaboration. The experts we spoke with were emphatic about this. The person being cared for should be fully informed as to what devices are being used and where, what they do, and how they work. And the caregiver should explain how these devices can help them feel safer and more comfortable, and even live a more independent life. Also, the decision to use technology necessarily raises security and privacy concerns. At Wirecutter, we vet all of our device and service picks to confirm that they abide by standard safety practices. Whatever privacy sacrifices you may incur by using the devices and services we recommend, we believe that, based on our experience, the real-world benefits outweigh any likely risks. “I don’t worry about privacy, because it’s already gone straight to hell,” Anne Madison told us. “These people are smarter than I am. I just don’t think about it much. I’m more worried about turning on my lights. Claire Perlman, Wirecutter’s editor of accessibility and aging in place, noted that it’s important to recognize that most devices are not one-size-fits-all. “The spectrum of ages and abilities of those aging in place varies so widely that it’s difficult to choose just one device for such a broad community,” she said. “Is the goal to help the older person control their immediate environment or the ambience of their home? To help a long-distance caretaker know that their loved one is okay? I think it depends on the individual’s goals more than anything else.” For each section of this guide, we looked for devices that offered the following features and attributes:  \tSimple operation: Ideally, tech devices are intuitive to use and allow operation via some combination of manual controls, apps, or voice commands.  \tWireless connectivity: Most of these devices require a Wi-Fi connection to function. If you’re planning to use multiple devices or any that stream video, first confirm that the person’s home has sufficiently speedy Wi-Fi. If the home has reception issues, consider investing in a wireless extender or a Wi-Fi mesh-networking kit.  \tSafety: We considered a lot of different types of devices for this guide but focused primarily on those that enhance safety and security, factors that are of particular concern for older people living at home and especially those living alone.  \tUnobtrusive design: We looked for devices that wouldn’t clutter a home and could function without interrupting everyday life. That included devices that could work in a traditional manner, such as a light switch or doorbell, while also supporting smart features.  \tSmart-home functions: Many of the devices we selected work with iOS or Android devices, but if you intend to have several devices work together, first confirm that they support the same smart-home software platforms. The most popular platforms are Amazon Alexa, Apple Home, Google Home, Matter, and Samsung SmartThings. You can find tech devices to help with everything from hearing sounds to taking daily medications to ensuring that someone is staying active and out of danger. And if you’re a caregiver, these devices make it easier to monitor loved ones, whether they’re on the other side of town or the other side of the country. Emergency-contact systems How it helps: According to the CDC Foundation, more than one in four people 65 and older fall each year. It’s the leading cause of injury for that age group, with over 38,000 deaths recorded from falls in 2021 alone. Having a way to contact emergency services is an absolute must. “A device that can be worn on the wrist allows for 24/7 monitoring and can help identify against the biggest and most common concern, which is a fall,” said David Siegel, CEO of Nationwide Medical. “A wrist-worn device can also provide important data, detect falls, determine location, and even serve as a telephonic or text communication device.” Why it’s a pick: The UnaliWear Kanega Medical Alert Watch doesn’t look like an emergency device, but it acts like one, providing on-the-go care both inside and outside the home. It relies on the Verizon network but also connects to Wi-Fi (and we found that the GPS worked best in that mode). When an emergency arises, just push a button or verbally tell the watch using set wake words; from there, you’re connected to a live operator. For times when you can’t do that, the Kanega watch has fall detection. In our testing, we found that agents would call a cell phone if the user couldn’t hear them clearly through the watch. The Kanega watch also includes medication reminders, and because it comes with a pair of swappable rechargeable batteries, you never have to worry about being without it for charging. The Kanega watch costs $300 (including the setup fee), plus a monthly service charge of nearly $80, which may seem high. However, it can be used anywhere within range of a cellular signal, not just in your home like the less expensive LifeFone Safe Watch Active (more information on that and other options below). How it helps: If you don’t like the idea of a wearable but want the assurance of easy access to assistance, consider Alexa Emergency Assist. This paid service ($6 per month or $59 per year) relies on your having an Amazon Echo voice-controlled smart speaker, through which you can connect to live responders who assist in calling emergency responders and relaying your medical information. Why it’s a pick: Of the medical alert systems we tested, Alexa Emergency Assist had the fastest response time. In addition to connecting with live emergency services, the service can notify up to 25 of your emergency contacts. Alexa Emergency Assist works with all Amazon Echo smart speakers and screens, but we like the Amazon Echo Dot for this purpose because it’s small and inexpensive. If you aren’t familiar with smart speakers, it may involve a bit of a learning curve, but once you get used to it, Alexa has other useful features, including the ability to act as an intercom, allowing family, friends, and caregivers with Echo devices or the Alexa app to “drop in” for an instant two-way conversation. Anne Madison has been using it since 2023. She told us that the service works well, and she’s “now a firm believer in it” after a fall in the bedroom left her unable to get up. “I said something along the lines of ’Alexa, call for help,’ and an actual human operator answered, listened to my problem, and indicated that they would call 911,” she said. Paramedics arrived within a few minutes and suggested that she call a relative. In the chaos, Anne misplaced her phone, but a quick “Alexa, call my phone” helped her locate it just as swiftly. How it helps: If you aren’t tech-savvy, know that there are still plenty of reliable emergency alert systems that let you call for help without having to deal with apps or smart speakers. These options are very straightforward and easy to set up. Some include a wearable device that goes on your wrist or around your neck; either option allows you to get help just by pushing a button so long as you are at home and in range of the wireless base station. Why it’s a pick: The LifeFone At-Home Cellular is an easy-to-use system with strong wireless coverage provided by AT\u0026amp;T’s cellular network. The system includes a base unit with a speaker and the option of a neck pendant or a wristband and is designed for use when you are at home (if you prefer a model that works everywhere, consider the UnaliWear Kanega Medical Alert Watch, above). We particularly like the pendant for people with limited dexterity or strength, because it has a magnetic clasp that’s still easy enough to use for those with dexterity limitations. If you need help, push a button on your wearable, and you’re quickly connected to an audio call with a live agent who can contact designated family, friends, and caregivers. In order to speak to the agent, though, you need to be in voice range of the base station. We also like that LifeFone has no setup or cancellation fees, but the service with one wearable pendant starts at $31 per month if you pay annually (otherwise it costs $35 per month). Another option to consider: The Apple Watch SE 3 combines the features of an iPhone with a smartwatch. It also includes built-in fall detection and crash detection, which you can enable to automatically alert emergency contacts or even 911 in case of an accident; you can also summon help by pressing a side button. In addition, you can set up the Apple Watch to share important medical information, such as allergies and medications, with emergency responders. While the cellular model works independently like a phone (and has its own number and cell plan), the less expensive base-model Apple Watch needs to be paired with an iPhone that’s always within Bluetooth range in order to send alerts or make calls; it can also connect to Wi-Fi, but it works that way only with known networks. Smart smoke alarm How it helps: According to the National Fire Protection Association, 59% of home fire deaths result from the absence of a smoke alarm or the presence of a faulty one. Everyone should have working smoke detectors throughout the house, but there’s a particular concern as to whether someone with reduced hearing will notice an alarm — and how quickly they might react to it. Smart smoke detectors not only deliver an audible siren inside the house but can also send alerts to friends, neighbors, and caregivers. Why it’s a pick: The Kidde Smart Smoke + Carbon Monoxide Alarm is a hardwired model that can detect both smoke and carbon monoxide. When the device detects danger, it sends a smartphone alert to the owners so that they are alerted while home or away, as well as to any other designated contacts. If it turns out that someone is just cooking up bacon, you can easily silence the alarm through the Kidde app. And because it interconnects with hardwired non-smart Kidde detectors, every detector in the house can have the same smart features. Note that this hardwired model still requires backup batteries (that’s a good thing) and issues an alert when those are getting low. Another option to consider: If you want emergency services to be available, consider the Kidde Ring Smart Smoke and CO Alarm. This model is almost identical to the above Kidde detector, except it works with the standalone $5-per-month 24/7 Smoke and CO Professional Monitoring plan or a Ring Protect plan (if you have the Ring Alarm). Unlike the other version, this model uses the Ring app. Also, you can’t silence it in the app: You need to push a physical button on the device (unless the smoke subsides first). Hearing aids How it helps: Prescription hearing aids are expensive, yet many are not covered by insurance. Over-the-counter hearing aids are a slightly more affordable option specifically designed for people who have mild to moderate hearing loss and are able to clearly communicate feedback. These devices don’t require a prescription and can be set up by the wearer, a family member, or a caregiver. Our headphones expert, writer Lauren Dragan, advises that anyone with more severe hearing loss or who needs sound processing that bypasses the inner ear should consult a medical professional instead of trying to use an OTC hearing aid. Why it’s a pick: The Jabra Enhance Select 300 is easy to set up, customize, and use, thanks to the Jabra app, which includes a video orientation section with an audio professional. We found this set to be more adjustable than most, with options to fine-tune the volume, as well as to balance treble, midrange, and bass. Instead of using replaceable button batteries, this set runs on rechargeable batteries, which provide about 15 hours of use. We found that the earpieces were good at staying in place and also provided a pretty comfortable fit. Another option to consider: If you need amplification in only one ear or a device for only occasional use, check out the Tweak Enhance. It’s available in single-ear form, and it ships with two pairs of 2B sound tubes and two sizes of domes for a good fit. Our panelists found its sound to be really good, but it isn’t as lightweight or feature-packed as other, more expensive options. Smart pill dispensers How it helps: If you take more than one pill a day, a pill box is a must. However, you have to fill the box weekly (or sometimes more often), and some people need an extra nudge to take their medications. A smart pill dispenser can organize several weeks’ worth of meds and can ensure that the right dose is being dispensed at precisely the right times. Why it’s a pick: The Hero Smart Pill Dispenser can hold up to 90 days’ worth of 10 different medications. When it’s pill-taking time, the Hero plays both audible and visual alerts. Then you simply press a button on the device itself to have your meds dispensed. If someone skips a day or a dose, the Hero sends a smartphone alert to the user but also to anyone else you designate, through the companion app. It can also remind users to take up to 10 additional meds that aren’t stored in the device. One downside is that the Hero requires a one-year commitment to a monthly $45 subscription (or $30 per month if you pay annually). Our tester found the Hero to be reliable and said that while the cost was covered by insurance, the price out of pocket would still be a lot less than paying someone to manage and monitor medications.","title":"Health and safety","slug":"health-and-safety","chapterModifiedDate":null,"chapterPublishedDate":null,"faqs":null,"footnotes":null,"sources":null},{"body":[{"type":"tag","name":"p","attribs":{},"children":[{"data":"The following devices are helpful for people who want to continue to live independently but are concerned about security or may need to be monitored a bit more closely.
