Whatever devices you decide on, older adults and caregivers should have a discussion in advance about what devices will be installed, what information they can collect, and who will have access to them and control of them. Home security system How it helps: Home security systems provide more than just protection from break-ins, as these DIY systems also work with a wide array of helpful devices that make it easier to keep tabs on your home overall. Such systems include relatively inconspicuous sensors that let you know when a door or window has been left open. Security cameras notify you when visitors of any sort come, and they also record when deliveries arrive. Some systems offer other helpful add-on devices, such as water-leak sensors, glass-break sensors, and panic buttons. Conveniently, the systems also allow you to manage all of these devices using a single smartphone app (instead of juggling several). We strongly recommend adding a professional monitoring plan to a security system so that in the event of an alarm, a live person from a call center will alert you directly — and if you’re unreachable, they’ll notify anyone else you’ve deemed an emergency contact. The call center can also contact the police or fire department if there’s a need, or if no one can be reached. Why it’s a pick: The Ring Alarm system can support almost every type of security add-on you can imagine, including a few options for door and window sensors, smoke and carbon monoxide detectors, and several kinds of hardwired and battery-operated doorbells and cameras. If you want something more advanced, the Ring Alarm Pro system can also improve Wi-Fi coverage in your home and includes emergency internet backup (for a fee) and up to 24 additional hours of backup power (via battery packs) in case of an outage. It also offers the biggest bang for your buck when it comes to professional monitoring ($20 per month or $200 per year). Another option to consider: If you aren’t ready for a whole-house system, the Aqara Door and Window Sensor is an easy-to-use, inexpensive alternative. These devices can keep tabs on doors, windows, and drawers. For instance, a sensor on a door can send smartphone alerts whenever someone enters or leaves, or if the door has been left open too long. The sensors do require the use of a plug-in device called an Aqara Hub, but it can support up to 128 sensors and also adds smart-home support so that you can pair it with other devices around the house. Smart video doorbell How it helps: A smart doorbell camera allows you to screen whoever comes to the door, from anywhere in the world, regardless of whether the visitor even rings the bell. When someone rings the bell, you hear it at home but also receive a smartphone notification, which allows you to view in real time who is at your door. But even if someone drops by just to deliver a package, motion sensors will still detect them and alert you. From there, you can opt to answer the door remotely or just keep a record of who is coming and going — and how long they hang around. “Video doorbells are great, especially if [the person living with them is] having mobility challenges,” said William Dale, MD, PhD, professor and chair of supportive care medicine at the Center for Cancer and Aging at City of Hope. “Maybe the house is a little big for them, or they have to be in a far part of the house, and getting all the way to the doors becomes a challenge.” Why it’s a pick: The TP-Link Tapo D130 Wired Video Doorbell delivers a clear 2K image and can distinguish whether the action happening in front of it is due to a person, animal, vehicle, package, or general motion. It even comes with a plug-in chime that you can place in any outlet anywhere in the house. It’s easy to use, and it’s also great for someone on a budget, because the hardware costs less than $50 and recordings can be stored to a microSD card for free. Or you can pay for cloud-based storage, starting at $3.50 per month for one camera, so you never have to worry about someone stealing the card or a power outage affecting your storage. Another option to consider: If you don’t have existing doorbell wiring, the TP-Link Tapo D225 is an excellent battery-operated 2K doorbell camera with many of the same features as the hardwired D130. At 5.91 by 1.97 by 1.52 inches, it's much more chunky than the D130. That's due to the internal battery, which lasts about four months, depending on how much activity it captures. Smart indoor cameras How it helps: An indoor camera provides peace of mind when you’re not home, whether you want to monitor your property or your pet. For caregivers, it allows you to remotely monitor home visits and movement, as well as whether a person is taking their medicine. And for families, it also allows you to monitor the level of care your loved one is receiving. “I have a lot of families with cameras,” said Vikki Levine, director of client services at Ezra Home Care. “In a positive way, it’s affecting the caliber of work that our caregivers are doing. They’re not getting away with sitting on their phone, because I’ll know about it faster than fast.” All the experts we spoke with were adamant that installing indoor security cameras needs to be a decision made between you, your loved one, and any outside help. “Transparency should be obvious. Nobody likes to be monitored without knowing,” said City of Hope’s Dale. “These kinds of things have to be really carefully thought about rather than just assumed that it’s okay.” Talk to the person living with the camera about why it’s important, how it works, and how you can use it to communicate, since most cameras come equipped with a speaker and microphone. Why it’s a pick: The TP-Link Tapo 2K QHD Security Camera Indoor/Outdoor C120 can distinguish between people, pets, vehicles, and general motion, but it’s also inexpensive to run. You can opt to use a microSD card and record 24/7 footage or just motion-activated events. It also supports cloud storage, so you never have to worry about accessing content if someone pulls the card or the plug; a Tapo Care plan starts at $3.50 per month for one camera and increases to $7 for two, $10.50 for three, and $12 for up to 10. Another option to consider: The Tapo C120 supports Alexa and Google Home but not Apple Home. If Apple Home is your preferred ecosystem, or if you want to use Apple HomeKit Secure Video (a free video-storage service that comes with paid iCloud plans starting at $1 per camera), we recommend the Eufy Indoor Cam C120. The Eufy camera has some of the same features as the TP-Link Tapo model but is a bit more expensive. Smart locks How it helps: From a security perspective, smart locks are especially handy because there are no keys to lose, and you can be notified whenever someone triggers the lock. You can also check the status of locks using your voice or an app, and you can program them to automatically lock after a set period, when you leave or arrive home, and at a set time every night. Jessica Guthrie, a caregiver and advocate in Fredericksburg, Virginia, said that smart locks help her care for her 77-year-old mom, allowing her to let in home-health aides whether she’s home or out of the house. “It sounds really simple. And I can also lock the door from afar,” she said. “All of that has been so critical.” Why it’s a pick: The Schlage Arrive Smart WiFi Deadbolt doesn’t have some of the bells and whistles of other smart locks, which we think makes it ideal for this purpose. The Arrive is about as easy as it gets to set up and use. It has top-tier hardware, plus built-in smarts that allow you to lock and unlock the door remotely, as well as to check whether the door has been left open. And if you or a loved one ever forgets or misplaces a key, you can use an app to unlock the door. Another option to consider: The Yale Approach Lock with Wi-Fi + Keypad adds smarts to your existing lock, replacing the thumb turn of your deadbolt with a mechanized one that can trigger the lock. It should work with nearly any existing single-cylinder deadbolt, and it comes with a wireless keypad for the outside of the door. To use the remote-control features, you need to plug the included Wi-Fi hub into any wall outlet. Water-leak sensors How it helps: About one in 67 US households each year files an insurance claim related to water-damage incidents, according to the Insurance Information Institute. Leaks often happen in hard-to-notice and even harder-to-reach areas, such as under a sink, behind the washing machine, or from the water heater in the basement. Smart water-leak sensors can help by sending you a smartphone alert the instant they detect water. The best models have a loud alarm and the ability to integrate with smart-home platforms so that you can receive smart alerts. We also prefer when alerts can be silenced in the app, so you don’t need to physically access the detector (or wade through water) to stop an alarm. Why it’s a pick: The YoLink Water Leak Sensor 4 requires the YoLink Hub, which you can buy separately or as part of a bundle with multiple sensors. The YoLink leak sensor is one of the few to have both top- and bottom-mounted sensor probes, which make it more versatile — in our tests, both sensing methods detected water within two seconds. If water touches probes on the sensor, it sends a signal to the hub, which emits an 87-decibel alarm and also sends a smartphone alert so you can take care of the issue. YoLink also sends a smartphone alert when the sensor has gone offline, which is very useful — after all, a sensor that can’t send alerts isn’t so useful. It also has an off timer in the app to ensure that the sensor won’t keep beeping endlessly after it detects water. If you don’t want to purchase a hub, or if you prefer Google Home compatibility, we recommend the Kidde Water Leak + Freeze Detector.","title":"Security","slug":"security","chapterModifiedDate":null,"chapterPublishedDate":null,"faqs":null,"footnotes":null,"sources":null},{"body":[{"type":"tag","name":"p","attribs":{},"children":[{"data":"For an older adult, monitoring what goes on inside and outside of the house can become more difficult, and you may need additional help with everyday tasks. The following devices may be especially useful for creating a safe environment, ensuring that older adults are taking care of themselves and are able to call for help. Smartphones How it helps: It goes without saying that a smartphone is an essential tool of modern life, and most of the devices in this guide require access to and frequent use of one. For instance, you may need a smartphone for setting up devices and connecting them to the internet, or for controlling smart-home devices via touch or voice commands. A phone that is as simple to use as it is reliable is a worthy investment, and that typically means the phone has a large, finger-friendly display that is easy to control and use. Why it’s a pick: The Apple iPhone 17 Pro Max has a larger screen size than any other current iPhone models, at a sprawling 6.9 inches diagonal. That, plus an increased brightness of 3,000 nits, makes the screen far easier to read. We also like the Max model because it has robust battery life of up to 39 hours if you’re playing video (35 hours if it’s streaming). Other features include a triple-lens rear camera, a sharp 2868×1320-pixel resolution, an always-on display, and a 120 Hz refresh rate for smoother scrolling. It also has a Ceramic Shield 2 coating to protect it from scratches and cracks, as well as an aluminum body to prevent it from overheating. How it helps: For Android fans, a feature-filled smartphone that stays current with software updates is essential. And one that has a large display, a trim weight, and lengthy battery life is the best possible combination. Why it’s a pick: The Google Pixel 10 Pro XL has an extra-large, 6.8-inch screen with a crisp 120 Hz refresh rate, so it makes photos, videos, and reading easier on the eyes than other models do. With its increased size comes a longer-life battery: In our tests, the 5,200 mAh battery (the biggest of all the Pixel phones) lasted through more than a day of use. The Pixel 10 Pro XL is also brighter than previous models, with 3,300 nits of peak brightness. Like the iPhone 17 Pro Max, this Android phone has three rear camera lenses. It also includes access to several AI-based features, such as Best Take, Add Me, and Gemini Live. Smart speakers and screens How it helps: Smart speakers and screens make keeping in touch easier and more personal than a simple phone call. These devices connect to your home’s Wi-Fi network, allowing for video and voice calls to family, friends, telemedicine appointments, and emergency services; you can also set alarms, reminders, and more. These devices are easy to control with voice commands, so you can call up news, weather forecasts, recipes, sports scores, and trivia, too. For homes with smart-home devices such as light bulbs, security cameras, and smart plugs, you can use a smart speaker to control them using your voice. Caregiver Kat Carney told us that she has always had problems waking up her 84-year-old mother. Then she purchased an Alexa device for their shared Marietta, Georgia, home. “She gets up when Alexa tells her to get up,” she said, noting that she also uses the devices to set Reminders about appointments and medications. “Alexa is my best friend.” Why it’s a pick: All Alexa speakers are controlled by voice commands — you can ask for the news, the weather, and sports scores, and you can stream music, radio, and audiobooks. In addition, they’re an especially useful way to control smart devices that otherwise may be inconvenient or challenging to interact with. The Amazon Echo Show 11 also has a 11-inch HD touchscreen, so you can see and read information as a supplement to voice controls and audio. On top of that, its 13-megapixel camera turns its screen into a straightforward video-chat device, so you can stay in touch with family, friends, and caregivers via Alexa Calling as well as third-party options like Zoom. The Echo Show also has a “drop-in” two-way video- or audio-chat feature for instant conversation that works just like a two-way intercom. (If only one party has an Alexa device, that’s okay: Video chats also work with the Alexa app on any smartphone.) How it helps: Several experts we spoke to highly recommended investing in a tablet because they are generally portable and can provide easy access to news, entertainment, and even the outside world via text, email, and video chat. A tablet can also be more intuitive and comfortable to use than a laptop, since it functions like a smartphone but with a much larger screen, so icons are easier to read, and touch controls are more forgiving. Why it’s a pick: If you’re accustomed to using a smartphone, you can easily transition to using an Apple iPad to access the apps for all of the devices we mention elsewhere in this guide. Using both Siri and an iPad, you can combine the control of multiple smart-home devices with one voice command: For instance, you can say “Siri, turn on the living room” to power several lights simultaneously. And you can combine those lights with smart plugs, cameras, and other smart devices, as long as they support the Apple Home platform. (Note, though, that Siri voice commands and the Home platform are not compatible with as many smart-home devices as Alexa and Google Home are.) Another option to consider: If you want all of the features of Alexa but don’t want a screen (or camera), opt for a smart speaker like the Amazon Echo Dot. If you prefer the look and feel of fabric, the company also makes ","type":"text"},{"type":"tag","name":"a","attribs":{"class":"product-link","href":"https://wclink.co/link/68414/235031/4/246018/?merchant=Amazon","rel":"noopener sponsored","target":"_blank","data-gtm-trigger":"product_link","data-store":"Amazon"},"children":[{"data":"smart curtains","type":"text"}]},{"data":".","type":"text"}]},{"data":"\n","type":"text"},{"type":"tag","name":"p","attribs":{},"children":[{"type":"tag","name":"em","attribs":{},"children":[{"data":"This article was edited by Jon Chase and Grant Clauser.","type":"text"}]}]},{"data":"\n","type":"text"}],"bodyContent":"As people age, it’s not uncommon for once everyday tasks to become more challenging and in some cases even impossible. The following devices are often recommended by both experts and older adults we’ve spoken with. Smart bulbs How it helps: Smart light bulbs are a quicker and less permanent option for smart lighting than smart switches and dimmers. Most of them look like standard bulbs and fit the same fixtures but provide smart-lighting features such as remote control and scheduling. Just keep in mind that if you turn the bulb off at the lamp or a switch, you can’t use an app, scheduling, or voice control — that restriction makes smart bulbs best for areas where you want them to turn on and off on a regular schedule. Why it’s a pick: The WiZ 60W A19 Color LED Smart Bulb uses Wi-Fi (so there’s no need for a wireless hub) and is easy to set up and control, yet it’s inexpensive enough that it allows you to add smart lighting throughout a home. You can dim it, tune it to different colors and white temperatures (warm white, daylight, or cool white), control it remotely, and put it on a schedule, all through the app. Smart plugs How it helps: Smart plugs let you transform the way you control traditional electrical devices, yet they are the most straightforward assistive devices around — just plug one into an outlet, pair it with a smartphone, and then plug a table lamp, fan, or other on/off device into the smart plug. You can turn any of those devices on or off using a smartphone app or a voice command to a smart speaker or your phone. Or, if you prefer, you can put those devices on a repeating schedule, just as you would a traditional lighting timer. Dan Kosmowski, who cares for his 79-year-old father in Birmingham, Michigan, said that his father often got distracted by the TV and forgot to use the bathroom. “Because of his type of dementia, he’s easily distracted,” he said. He uses a smart plug to make sure the TV stays off after-hours. “I tried using the built-in timer on the remote control, but I never remembered to do it consistently,” Kosmowski said. “So we have a recurring power-off cycle set for 10, and then it comes back on at 7 or 8 in the morning.” Why it’s a pick: The TP-Link Kasa Smart Wi-Fi Plug Slim (EP25) has everything you may want in a single-outlet smart plug, including remote control, easy-to-set timers, and scheduling. It’s also compatible with all of the major smart-home platforms for voice control. An extra feature for anyone who is conservation-minded is that it monitors the energy usage of whatever device you plug into it, displaying information in watts and kilowatt-hours (or hours and minutes) in the app. Smart window shades How it helps: Smart window shades are a godsend for anyone who has mobility or physical limitations. These shades are especially helpful if a dwelling has multiple windows, multiple floors, or windows in hard-to-reach places. You can manually control most models through an app or voice control; you can also put them on a daily schedule so that they automatically open every morning and close at dusk (or any other times you prefer). Why it’s a pick: SmartWings Motorized Light Filtering Roller Shades are easy to set up and use. They’re whisper-quiet, and they have an optional five-channel remote control for people who don’t have a phone or smart speaker handy. Unlike most models, SmartWings shades don’t have a dedicated control app. But because they’re Matter-enabled, you can control them using any of the major third-party platforms, such as Alexa, Apple Home, or Google Home (though that also means you’ll need a Matter hub, such as a smart speaker). The shades are available in a range of colors and styles, including blackout fabric that not only fully darkens a room but in our tests also helped regulate a room’s temperature. If you prefer the look and feel of fabric, the company also makes smart curtains. This article was edited by Jon Chase and Grant Clauser.","title":"Accessibility and
